I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always want to make it sound dramatic, but when I’m sitting in something where I’m supposed to stay put—like a client interview, or tutoring, or even book club—I start feeling like I need to get up. I’ll find some little reason to move instead of just sitting and listening. People have started noticing, which is… a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting when you're sitting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do… probably more than I’d like to admit. When I’m at my computer going through records, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen without really noticing. Sometimes I shift around so much that I actually lose my place in what I’m reading. I mean, I can usually pull myself back, but… yes, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I try to be pretty careful with details because of my work, so I wouldn’t want to say I’m making lots of careless mistakes if I’m not. I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem… at least not compared to the sitting-still thing. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure I can say “consistently” with confidence. I mean, I’ve always had a lot of energy and I tend to move quickly, but I’ve also built a life around work where that can look… useful, I guess.

I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t usually think of myself as someone who can’t focus — I mean, in my work, I can lock onto something if it feels urgent or important.

They’ve hinted that they feel sort of steamrolled by me. I don’t mean to do that—I’m trying to be useful—but I can see how it’s affecting things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty sitting still and acting on impulse, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, if I’m being honest. Not that I was a “bad kid” or anything… I did well enough, I was considered bright. But my grade-school report cards apparently kept saying things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or had trouble waiting my turn.\n\nMy mother still teases me that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I needed to pace through. So… yes, I suppose it’s not exactly new. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean to take over, but I’ll start talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting, and before I know it I’ve sort of filled up all the space.\n\nSometimes the other person is giving pretty clear signs they’re done—looking away, trying to wrap it up—and I still keep going because one more thought occurs to me. It’s embarrassing when I notice it afterward. Is that what you mean by dominating conversations?\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to something as clearly. I don’t want to overstate it. The place it’s really showing up is professionally — I’ve missed details in investigations, irritated clients by pushing too fast, and I think some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n\nSo, personally… maybe it spills over, but the concrete problems I can name are mostly at work. Is that still useful to tell you?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Maybe not “running and climbing” in some wild way… at least, I don’t think that’s how my family would put it. But yes, I was apparently very physically restless.\n\nMy grade-school report cards kept saying I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So… I suppose the answer is yes, just maybe more pacing and being constantly up than climbing on things. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “consistently” with confidence. I mean, I’ve always had a lot of energy and I tend to move quickly, but I’ve also built a life around work where that can look… useful, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t want to over-answer. It feels like it’s been part of me for a long time, but I’m not sure I’ve tracked it clearly throughout adulthood. Is that the kind of answer you need, or should I be thinking about specific periods?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t usually think of myself as someone who can’t focus — I mean, in my work, I can lock onto something if it feels urgent or important.\n\nBut with ordinary tasks, or things that are slower or more routine… I might drift, yes. I’ll reread the same thing or realize I’ve been half-listening. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” though. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of focus difficulty you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not totally sure. I mean, I notice things around me — it’s part of my job, so I’ve always thought of that as being attentive rather than distracted.

I’d like to say no, or at least “not significantly,” because I do try very hard not to take things out on people close to me.

But… if I’m being honest, the bigger problems have shown up professionally more than with friends or family. I mean, I can usually pull myself back, but… yes, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I try to be pretty careful with details because of my work, so I wouldn’t want to say I’m making lots of careless mistakes if I’m not. I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem… at least not compared to the sitting-still thing. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless, exactly… but yes, I do jump in before I’ve really thought it through sometimes. Like if I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor having a conversation in the hallway, I’ll just start asking questions or offering advice before anyone’s invited me in.\n\nAnd then a minute later I realize, oh… I kind of barged into that. I don’t mean it badly—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it might come across. Is that the kind of impulse you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just annoying, but… yes, it is starting to cause problems. Professionally, mostly. I’ve missed details in investigations because I’m rushing ahead or can’t settle long enough with something, and I’ve had a few clients get irritated because I jump in too quickly or push the conversation too hard.\n\nAnd with the junior detectives… I think some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ve hinted that they feel sort of steamrolled by me. I don’t mean to do that—I’m trying to be useful—but I can see how it’s affecting things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty sitting still and acting on impulse, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, if I’m being honest. Not that I was a “bad kid” or anything… I did well enough, I was considered bright. But my grade-school report cards apparently kept saying things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or had trouble waiting my turn.\n\nMy mother still teases me that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I needed to pace through. So… yes, I suppose it’s not exactly new. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean to take over, but I’ll start talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting, and before I know it I’ve sort of filled up all the space.\n\nSometimes the other person is giving pretty clear signs they’re done—looking away, trying to wrap it up—and I still keep going because one more thought occurs to me. It’s embarrassing when I notice it afterward. Is that what you mean by dominating conversations?\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to something as clearly. I don’t want to overstate it. The place it’s really showing up is professionally — I’ve missed details in investigations, irritated clients by pushing too fast, and I think some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n\nSo, personally… maybe it spills over, but the concrete problems I can name are mostly at work. Is that still useful to tell you?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Maybe not “running and climbing” in some wild way… at least, I don’t think that’s how my family would put it. But yes, I was apparently very physically restless.\n\nMy grade-school report cards kept saying I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So… I suppose the answer is yes, just maybe more pacing and being constantly up than climbing on things. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “consistently” with confidence. I mean, I’ve always had a lot of energy and I tend to move quickly, but I’ve also built a life around work where that can look… useful, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t want to over-answer. It feels like it’s been part of me for a long time, but I’m not sure I’ve tracked it clearly throughout adulthood. Is that the kind of answer you need, or should I be thinking about specific periods?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t usually think of myself as someone who can’t focus — I mean, in my work, I can lock onto something if it feels urgent or important.\n\nBut with ordinary tasks, or things that are slower or more routine… I might drift, yes. I’ll reread the same thing or realize I’ve been half-listening. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” though. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of focus difficulty you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant restlessness and impulsivity since childhood that has continued into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it does seem like it’s been there since I was young.\n\nLike I mentioned, my grade-school report cards always said I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through.\n\nSo yes, I suppose there’s a pretty clear thread there — at least with the restlessness and the jumping ahead before my turn. Is that the kind of lifelong pattern you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I do finish the important things, especially if someone’s depending on me.\n\nThere are times with instructions, though, where if they’re detailed or kind of tedious, I’ll realize I missed a step or I have to go back and check what was actually asked. But I don’t know if that’s losing focus exactly, or just trying to move too quickly.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I notice things around me — it’s part of my job, so I’ve always thought of that as being attentive rather than distracted.\n\nBut if you mean whether noises or movement pull me off what I’m doing… sometimes, yes, probably. I can be mid-task and suddenly I’m tracking a conversation nearby or something happening outside the window. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoes that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your personal relationships, such as with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, or at least “not significantly,” because I do try very hard not to take things out on people close to me.\n\nBut… if I’m being honest, the bigger problems have shown up professionally more than with friends or family. I’ve missed details in investigations because I was moving too fast, and I’ve irritated clients by interrupting or pushing ahead before they’re finished explaining. And some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me now, I think because they feel like I steamroll them.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to be difficult — I’m usually trying to get to the point or help solve the thing faster — but I can see how it’s started costing me. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strain in your friendships or romantic relationships because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in any big, obvious way. I mean, people close to me know I can be… energetic, maybe impatient sometimes, but I don’t think I’ve lost friendships over it or anything like that.\n\nRomantically, I’m not sure. There have probably been moments where someone felt I wasn’t settling down or I was moving too fast in a conversation, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s not something anyone has sat me down and said, “This is a major problem.”\n\nIs that the kind of strain you mean, or are you asking about more serious conflict?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s not something anyone has sat me down and said, “This is a major problem.”\n\nIs that the kind of strain you mean, or are you asking about more serious conflict?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a romantic relationship end, in part or fully, because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… don’t think so. Not that I can point to and say, “Yes, that relationship ended because I was too restless or impulsive.”

I’m sure I’ve had moments where I was impatient, or maybe hard to settle with, but breakups are usually complicated, aren’t they? I wouldn’t want to blame it on that unless someone had actually said that to me.

So my honest answer is probably no, or at least not that I’m aware of. Does that answer it clearly enough?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a harder time staying seated when I’m supposed to. It’s probably not a huge thing, but it’s becoming noticeable—like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. I keep finding reasons to get up instead of just sitting and listening, and people are starting to pick up on it. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always want to make it sound dramatic, but when I’m sitting in something where I’m supposed to stay put—like a client interview, or tutoring, or even book club—I start feeling like I need to get up. I’ll find some little reason to move instead of just sitting and listening. People have started noticing, which is… a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting when you're sitting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do… probably more than I’d like to admit. When I’m at my computer going through records, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen without really noticing. Sometimes I shift around so much that I actually lose my place in what I’m reading. I mean, I can usually pull myself back, but… yes, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I try to be pretty careful with details because of my work, so I wouldn’t want to say I’m making lots of careless mistakes if I’m not. I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem… at least not compared to the sitting-still thing. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless, exactly… but yes, I do jump in before I’ve really thought it through sometimes. Like if I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor having a conversation in the hallway, I’ll just start asking questions or offering advice before anyone’s invited me in.\n\nAnd then a minute later I realize, oh… I kind of barged into that. I don’t mean it badly—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it might come across. Is that the kind of impulse you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just annoying, but… yes, it is starting to cause problems. Professionally, mostly. I’ve missed details in investigations because I’m rushing ahead or can’t settle long enough with something, and I’ve had a few clients get irritated because I jump in too quickly or push the conversation too hard.\n\nAnd with the junior detectives… I think some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ve hinted that they feel sort of steamrolled by me. I don’t mean to do that—I’m trying to be useful—but I can see how it’s affecting things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty sitting still and acting on impulse, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, if I’m being honest. Not that I was a “bad kid” or anything… I did well enough, I was considered bright. But my grade-school report cards apparently kept saying things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or had trouble waiting my turn.\n\nMy mother still teases me that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I needed to pace through. So… yes, I suppose it’s not exactly new. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean to take over, but I’ll start talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting, and before I know it I’ve sort of filled up all the space.\n\nSometimes the other person is giving pretty clear signs they’re done—looking away, trying to wrap it up—and I still keep going because one more thought occurs to me. It’s embarrassing when I notice it afterward. Is that what you mean by dominating conversations?\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to something as clearly. I don’t want to overstate it. The place it’s really showing up is professionally — I’ve missed details in investigations, irritated clients by pushing too fast, and I think some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n\nSo, personally… maybe it spills over, but the concrete problems I can name are mostly at work. Is that still useful to tell you?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Maybe not “running and climbing” in some wild way… at least, I don’t think that’s how my family would put it. But yes, I was apparently very physically restless.\n\nMy grade-school report cards kept saying I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So… I suppose the answer is yes, just maybe more pacing and being constantly up than climbing on things. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “consistently” with confidence. I mean, I’ve always had a lot of energy and I tend to move quickly, but I’ve also built a life around work where that can look… useful, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t want to over-answer. It feels like it’s been part of me for a long time, but I’m not sure I’ve tracked it clearly throughout adulthood. Is that the kind of answer you need, or should I be thinking about specific periods?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t usually think of myself as someone who can’t focus — I mean, in my work, I can lock onto something if it feels urgent or important.\n\nBut with ordinary tasks, or things that are slower or more routine… I might drift, yes. I’ll reread the same thing or realize I’ve been half-listening. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” though. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of focus difficulty you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant restlessness and impulsivity since childhood that has continued into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it does seem like it’s been there since I was young.\n\nLike I mentioned, my grade-school report cards always said I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through.\n\nSo yes, I suppose there’s a pretty clear thread there — at least with the restlessness and the jumping ahead before my turn. Is that the kind of lifelong pattern you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I do finish the important things, especially if someone’s depending on me.\n\nThere are times with instructions, though, where if they’re detailed or kind of tedious, I’ll realize I missed a step or I have to go back and check what was actually asked. But I don’t know if that’s losing focus exactly, or just trying to move too quickly.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I notice things around me — it’s part of my job, so I’ve always thought of that as being attentive rather than distracted.\n\nBut if you mean whether noises or movement pull me off what I’m doing… sometimes, yes, probably. I can be mid-task and suddenly I’m tracking a conversation nearby or something happening outside the window. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoes that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your personal relationships, such as with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, or at least “not significantly,” because I do try very hard not to take things out on people close to me.\n\nBut… if I’m being honest, the bigger problems have shown up professionally more than with friends or family. I’ve missed details in investigations because I was moving too fast, and I’ve irritated clients by interrupting or pushing ahead before they’re finished explaining. And some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me now, I think because they feel like I steamroll them.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to be difficult — I’m usually trying to get to the point or help solve the thing faster — but I can see how it’s started costing me. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strain in your friendships or romantic relationships because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in any big, obvious way. It’s not something anyone has sat me down and said, “This is a major problem.”\n\nIs that the kind of strain you mean, or are you asking about more serious conflict?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a romantic relationship end, in part or fully, because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so. There have probably been moments where someone felt I wasn’t settling down or I was moving too fast in a conversation, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s not something anyone has sat me down and said, “This is a major problem.”\n\nIs that the kind of strain you mean, or are you asking about more serious conflict?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a romantic relationship end, in part or fully, because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Across both… I’d say it’s much clearer at work than in my personal life.

I don’t want to make it sound catastrophic, because I do still function and I care a lot about doing the job well. But it is starting to cost me professionally. I’ve missed details in investigations because I moved too fast, I’ve irritated clients by cutting in or pushing ahead, and I think some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a harder time staying seated when I’m supposed to. It’s probably not a huge thing, but it’s becoming noticeable—like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. I keep finding reasons to get up instead of just sitting and listening, and people are starting to pick up on it. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always want to make it sound dramatic, but when I’m sitting in something where I’m supposed to stay put—like a client interview, or tutoring, or even book club—I start feeling like I need to get up. I’ll find some little reason to move instead of just sitting and listening. People have started noticing, which is… a little embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting when you're sitting?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do… probably more than I’d like to admit. When I’m at my computer going through records, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen without really noticing. Sometimes I shift around so much that I actually lose my place in what I’m reading. I mean, I can usually pull myself back, but… yes, it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I try to be pretty careful with details because of my work, so I wouldn’t want to say I’m making lots of careless mistakes if I’m not. I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem… at least not compared to the sitting-still thing. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless, exactly… but yes, I do jump in before I’ve really thought it through sometimes. Like if I overhear junior detectives talking through a lead, or even a neighbor having a conversation in the hallway, I’ll just start asking questions or offering advice before anyone’s invited me in.\n\nAnd then a minute later I realize, oh… I kind of barged into that. I don’t mean it badly—I’m usually trying to help—but I can see how it might come across. Is that the kind of impulse you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it’s just annoying, but… yes, it is starting to cause problems. Professionally, mostly. I’ve missed details in investigations because I’m rushing ahead or can’t settle long enough with something, and I’ve had a few clients get irritated because I jump in too quickly or push the conversation too hard.\n\nAnd with the junior detectives… I think some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ve hinted that they feel sort of steamrolled by me. I don’t mean to do that—I’m trying to be useful—but I can see how it’s affecting things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty sitting still and acting on impulse, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, if I’m being honest. Not that I was a “bad kid” or anything… I did well enough, I was considered bright. But my grade-school report cards apparently kept saying things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or had trouble waiting my turn.\n\nMy mother still teases me that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I needed to pace through. So… yes, I suppose it’s not exactly new. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean to take over, but I’ll start talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting, and before I know it I’ve sort of filled up all the space.\n\nSometimes the other person is giving pretty clear signs they’re done—looking away, trying to wrap it up—and I still keep going because one more thought occurs to me. It’s embarrassing when I notice it afterward. Is that what you mean by dominating conversations?\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness causing problems in your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to something as clearly. I don’t want to overstate it. The place it’s really showing up is professionally — I’ve missed details in investigations, irritated clients by pushing too fast, and I think some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n\nSo, personally… maybe it spills over, but the concrete problems I can name are mostly at work. Is that still useful to tell you?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Maybe not “running and climbing” in some wild way… at least, I don’t think that’s how my family would put it. But yes, I was apparently very physically restless.\n\nMy grade-school report cards kept saying I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. So… I suppose the answer is yes, just maybe more pacing and being constantly up than climbing on things. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently throughout your adult life as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “consistently” with confidence. I mean, I’ve always had a lot of energy and I tend to move quickly, but I’ve also built a life around work where that can look… useful, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t want to over-answer. It feels like it’s been part of me for a long time, but I’m not sure I’ve tracked it clearly throughout adulthood. Is that the kind of answer you need, or should I be thinking about specific periods?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t usually think of myself as someone who can’t focus — I mean, in my work, I can lock onto something if it feels urgent or important.\n\nBut with ordinary tasks, or things that are slower or more routine… I might drift, yes. I’ll reread the same thing or realize I’ve been half-listening. I don’t know if that counts as “often,” though. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of focus difficulty you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant restlessness and impulsivity since childhood that has continued into your adult life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it does seem like it’s been there since I was young.\n\nLike I mentioned, my grade-school report cards always said I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through.\n\nSo yes, I suppose there’s a pretty clear thread there — at least with the restlessness and the jumping ahead before my turn. Is that the kind of lifelong pattern you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I do finish the important things, especially if someone’s depending on me.\n\nThere are times with instructions, though, where if they’re detailed or kind of tedious, I’ll realize I missed a step or I have to go back and check what was actually asked. But I don’t know if that’s losing focus exactly, or just trying to move too quickly.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I notice things around me — it’s part of my job, so I’ve always thought of that as being attentive rather than distracted.\n\nBut if you mean whether noises or movement pull me off what I’m doing… sometimes, yes, probably. I can be mid-task and suddenly I’m tracking a conversation nearby or something happening outside the window. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoes that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your personal relationships, such as with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, or at least “not significantly,” because I do try very hard not to take things out on people close to me.\n\nBut… if I’m being honest, the bigger problems have shown up professionally more than with friends or family. I’ve missed details in investigations because I was moving too fast, and I’ve irritated clients by interrupting or pushing ahead before they’re finished explaining. And some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me now, I think because they feel like I steamroll them.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strain in your friendships or romantic relationships because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in any big, obvious way. But I don’t know if that’s losing focus exactly, or just trying to move too quickly.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I notice things around me — it’s part of my job, so I’ve always thought of that as being attentive rather than distracted.\n\nBut if you mean whether noises or movement pull me off what I’m doing… sometimes, yes, probably. I can be mid-task and suddenly I’m tracking a conversation nearby or something happening outside the window. It’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoes that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your personal relationships, such as with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, or at least “not significantly,” because I do try very hard not to take things out on people close to me.\n\nBut… if I’m being honest, the bigger problems have shown up professionally more than with friends or family. I’ve missed details in investigations because I was moving too fast, and I’ve irritated clients by interrupting or pushing ahead before they’re finished explaining. And some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me now, I think because they feel like I steamroll them.\n\nThat’s embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to be difficult — I’m usually trying to get to the point or help solve the thing faster — but I can see how it’s started costing me. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strain in your friendships or romantic relationships because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so in any big, obvious way. I mean, people close to me know I can be… energetic, maybe impatient sometimes, but I don’t think I’ve lost friendships over it or anything like that.\n\nRomantically, I’m not sure. There have probably been moments where someone felt I wasn’t settling down or I was moving too fast in a conversation, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s not something anyone has sat me down and said, “This is a major problem.”\n\nIs that the kind of strain you mean, or are you asking about more serious conflict?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a romantic relationship end, in part or fully, because of your impulsivity or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so. Not that I can point to and say, “Yes, that relationship ended because I was too restless or impulsive.”\n\nI’m sure I’ve had moments where I was impatient, or maybe hard to settle with, but breakups are usually complicated, aren’t they? I wouldn’t want to blame it on that unless someone had actually said that to me.\n\nSo my honest answer is probably no, or at least not that I’m aware of. Does that answer it clearly enough?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity have consistently affected your performance or relationships across both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Across both… I’d say it’s much clearer at work than in my personal life.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound catastrophic, because I do still function and I care a lot about doing the job well. But it is starting to cost me professionally. I’ve missed details in investigations because I moved too fast, I’ve irritated clients by cutting in or pushing ahead, and I think some of the junior detectives are less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n\nPersonally, I don’t think it’s been as consistent or obvious. So I’d answer: yes, there’s a real effect, but mostly in my work performance and work relationships. Does that distinction make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
